I don’t know if I’d say I “enjoy” it exactly. I mean, sometimes if something tastes good, sure, I can recognize that it tastes good. Like if someone made something really nice or if there’s a specific thing I’m in the mood for, I’m not saying I’m incapable of liking flavors or anything.

I mean… I guess, yeah, a little. I don’t really weigh myself all the time or anything, so I don’t have some exact number to give you, but I’ve noticed my work pants are definitely looser than they used to be.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… “worried” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess I am. Not like I sit around thinking about numbers all day, but if I eat something that feels heavy—like pizza, or a really big sandwich, something like that—I get this really uncomfortable panicky feeling afterward.

It’s like my brain goes straight to, okay, tomorrow I’m going to wake up and feel bigger, heavier, slower. And I know that probably sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels really real. I start thinking about work, like being up on ladders or squeezing around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans and trying to move quickly, and I just picture myself not being able to move the way I need to. Like I’ll be sluggish or clumsy or just… too much in my own body.

So I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult about food. It’s more that certain foods make me feel like I’m going to lose control of how I feel in my body, and then I get tense about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do, probably more than I want to admit. It’s not always like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but when I’m deciding what to eat, I definitely think about how it’s going to make me feel in my body.

Like if it’s something heavier—pizza, or a big sandwich, or something really filling—I’ll hesitate. I start thinking, if I eat that, am I going to feel bigger afterward? Am I going to wake up tomorrow feeling heavy or slow? And then it sort of spirals from there, because my work is so physical. I’m on ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit around furniture and drop cloths and paint cans, and I hate the idea of feeling like my body is in the way.

So yeah, weight is part of it. Maybe I don’t always call it that in my head, but that’s what it is. I’ll choose something lighter because it feels safer, like I’ll still be able to move the way I need to. With heavier food, I get this panicky feeling, like one meal is going to somehow make me bigger and slower, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.

Like, the ones I wear on job sites, they kind of hang on me now, and I’m always pulling them up or tightening my belt more than I remember having to.\n\nAnd people have been commenting, which is honestly kind of irritating. Like coworkers or people on jobs will say, “You look too thin,” or ask if I’m eating enough, and I don’t really know what they want me to say to that. I feel like I’m just on my feet all day, climbing ladders, carrying stuff, moving around, so to me it makes sense that I’ve just… leaned out some. It doesn’t feel like this big dramatic thing the way other people make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess I am. Not like I sit around thinking about numbers all day, but if I eat something that feels heavy—like pizza, or a really big sandwich, something like that—I get this really uncomfortable panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s like my brain goes straight to, okay, tomorrow I’m going to wake up and feel bigger, heavier, slower. And I know that probably sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels really real. I start thinking about work, like being up on ladders or squeezing around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans and trying to move quickly, and I just picture myself not being able to move the way I need to. Like I’ll be sluggish or clumsy or just… too much in my own body.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult about food. It’s more that certain foods make me feel like I’m going to lose control of how I feel in my body, and then I get tense about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when deciding what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, probably more than I want to admit. It’s not always like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but when I’m deciding what to eat, I definitely think about how it’s going to make me feel in my body.\n\nLike if it’s something heavier—pizza, or a big sandwich, or something really filling—I’ll hesitate. I start thinking, if I eat that, am I going to feel bigger afterward? Am I going to wake up tomorrow feeling heavy or slow? And then it sort of spirals from there, because my work is so physical. I’m on ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit around furniture and drop cloths and paint cans, and I hate the idea of feeling like my body is in the way.\n\nSo yeah, weight is part of it. Maybe I don’t always call it that in my head, but that’s what it is. I’ll choose something lighter because it feels safer, like I’ll still be able to move the way I need to. With heavier food, I get this panicky feeling, like one meal is going to somehow make me bigger and slower, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “I’m skipping food because I’m scared of gaining weight,” because that sounds really blunt, but that’s kind of what it comes down to.

If the only thing around is something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I’ll put it off or just not have it. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ll say I’ll eat later, but really it’s because I get this panicky feeling like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Bigger and slower. And then I start thinking about work, being on ladders, moving through rooms, bending around furniture and buckets and drop cloths, and I just picture myself feeling clumsy or heavy in my body.

I know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t change everything overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like just one meal. It feels like I’m risking losing the way I can move, if that makes sense. So yeah, I do avoid eating sometimes when food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavier.

I didn’t really think it was something I needed a whole appointment for, but they were pretty insistent.\n\nMost days I just keep things simple, you know? Like I’ll have coffee in the morning, especially if I’m heading to a job early, and then maybe I’ll grab a few bites of something between painting jobs if there’s time or if I remember. And then dinner is usually small, if I’m not too busy with class paperwork or too tired by the time I get home. It’s not like I’m trying to make it complicated—I just sort of have a routine, and food ends up being… not the main thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy eating when you do have the time for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “enjoy” it exactly. I mean, sometimes if something tastes good, sure, I can recognize that it tastes good. Like if someone made something really nice or if there’s a specific thing I’m in the mood for, I’m not saying I’m incapable of liking flavors or anything.\n\nBut most of the time eating feels more like… a task? Like something I have to fit in around everything else. And sometimes by the time I sit down, I’m already thinking about what I still need to do, or whether I have enough time before I have to prep for the next job or finish paperwork, so it’s hard to really relax and enjoy it. I guess I don’t pay attention to it the way other people seem to. People talk about food like it’s this big enjoyable thing, and for me it’s just… I don’t know, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess, yeah, a little. I don’t really weigh myself all the time or anything, so I don’t have some exact number to give you, but I’ve noticed my work pants are definitely looser than they used to be. Like coworkers or people on jobs will say, “You look too thin,” or ask if I’m eating enough, and I don’t really know what they want me to say to that. I feel like I’m just on my feet all day, climbing ladders, carrying stuff, moving around, so to me it makes sense that I’ve just… leaned out some. It doesn’t feel like this big dramatic thing the way other people make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess I am. Not like I sit around thinking about numbers all day, but if I eat something that feels heavy—like pizza, or a really big sandwich, something like that—I get this really uncomfortable panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s like my brain goes straight to, okay, tomorrow I’m going to wake up and feel bigger, heavier, slower. And I know that probably sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels really real. I start thinking about work, like being up on ladders or squeezing around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans and trying to move quickly, and I just picture myself not being able to move the way I need to. Like I’ll be sluggish or clumsy or just… too much in my own body.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult about food. It’s more that certain foods make me feel like I’m going to lose control of how I feel in my body, and then I get tense about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when deciding what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, probably more than I want to admit. It’s not always like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but when I’m deciding what to eat, I definitely think about how it’s going to make me feel in my body.\n\nLike if it’s something heavier—pizza, or a big sandwich, or something really filling—I’ll hesitate. I start thinking, if I eat that, am I going to feel bigger afterward? Am I going to wake up tomorrow feeling heavy or slow? And then it sort of spirals from there, because my work is so physical. I’m on ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit around furniture and drop cloths and paint cans, and I hate the idea of feeling like my body is in the way.\n\nSo yeah, weight is part of it. And then I start thinking about work, being on ladders, moving through rooms, bending around furniture and buckets and drop cloths, and I just picture myself feeling clumsy or heavy in my body.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t change everything overnight. Yeah… I think it probably is. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because I’ll dress it up as, like, “I just don’t want to feel sluggish,” or “I need to be able to move for work,” but underneath that, yeah, it’s the fear of gaining weight or feeling bigger.

Like if I’m looking at something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something really filling—I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a lot of food.” I get this panicky feeling, like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up the next day bigger and slower somehow. And then I picture myself at work, trying to get around ladders and drop cloths and furniture, or squeezing through rooms with paint cans and all my stuff, and I feel like my body won’t move the way I need it to.

So yes, I guess that is the main reason I restrict. It’s not that I’m trying to make some big statement about food. It’s more like I’m trying to avoid that panic, that feeling that I’m going to become heavier and not be able to function right in my body.

And then dinner is usually small, if I’m not too busy with class paperwork or too tired by the time I get home. It’s not like I’m trying to make it complicated—I just sort of have a routine, and food ends up being… not the main thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy eating when you do have the time for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “enjoy” it exactly. I mean, sometimes if something tastes good, sure, I can recognize that it tastes good. Like something I have to fit in around everything else. I guess I don’t pay attention to it the way other people seem to. People talk about food like it’s this big enjoyable thing, and for me it’s just… I don’t know, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess, yeah, a little. I don’t really weigh myself all the time or anything, so I don’t have some exact number to give you, but I’ve noticed my work pants are definitely looser than they used to be. Like, the ones I wear on job sites, they kind of hang on me now, and I’m always pulling them up or tightening my belt more than I remember having to.\n\nAnd people have been commenting, which is honestly kind of irritating. Like coworkers or people on jobs will say, “You look too thin,” or ask if I’m eating enough, and I don’t really know what they want me to say to that. I feel like I’m just on my feet all day, climbing ladders, carrying stuff, moving around, so to me it makes sense that I’ve just… leaned out some. It doesn’t feel like this big dramatic thing the way other people make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess I am. Not like I sit around thinking about numbers all day, but if I eat something that feels heavy—like pizza, or a really big sandwich, something like that—I get this really uncomfortable panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s like my brain goes straight to, okay, tomorrow I’m going to wake up and feel bigger, heavier, slower. And I know that probably sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels really real. I start thinking about work, like being up on ladders or squeezing around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans and trying to move quickly, and I just picture myself not being able to move the way I need to. It’s more that certain foods make me feel like I’m going to lose control of how I feel in my body, and then I get tense about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when deciding what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, probably more than I want to admit. It’s not always like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but when I’m deciding what to eat, I definitely think about how it’s going to make me feel in my body.\n\nLike if it’s something heavier—pizza, or a big sandwich, or something really filling—I’ll hesitate. I start thinking, if I eat that, am I going to feel bigger afterward? Am I going to wake up tomorrow feeling heavy or slow? And then it sort of spirals from there, because my work is so physical. I’m on ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit around furniture and drop cloths and paint cans, and I hate the idea of feeling like my body is in the way.\n\nSo yeah, weight is part of it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I don’t think I look overweight now. I mean, I know other people act like my weight is this huge, urgent problem, but when I look at myself, that’s not what I see.

To me it feels like people are making a really big deal out of something that’s more about me being disciplined, or being busy, or just keeping myself able to work the way I need to. I’m on my feet all day, I’m moving around, and I don’t want to feel weighed down. So when someone says I’m too thin or they act alarmed, it’s hard for me to really take that in, because in my head it doesn’t feel like some emergency.

I’m not saying I think I’m overweight exactly. It’s more like… I don’t see what everyone else seems to be so worried about. I feel like they’re looking at me and deciding it’s worse than it is.

I mean, I don’t always think of it as “I’m skipping food because I’m scared of gaining weight,” because that sounds really blunt, but that’s kind of what it comes down to.\n\nIf the only thing around is something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I’ll put it off or just not have it. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ll say I’ll eat later, but really it’s because I get this panicky feeling like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Bigger and slower. And then I start thinking about work, being on ladders, moving through rooms, bending around furniture and buckets and drop cloths, and I just picture myself feeling clumsy or heavy in my body.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t change everything overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like just one meal. It feels like I’m risking losing the way I can move, if that makes sense. So yeah, I do avoid eating sometimes when food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavier.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because I’ll dress it up as, like, “I just don’t want to feel sluggish,” or “I need to be able to move for work,” but underneath that, yeah, it’s the fear of gaining weight or feeling bigger.\n\nLike if I’m looking at something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something really filling—I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a lot of food.” I get this panicky feeling, like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up the next day bigger and slower somehow. And then I picture myself at work, trying to get around ladders and drop cloths and furniture, or squeezing through rooms with paint cans and all my stuff, and I feel like my body won’t move the way I need it to.\n\nSo yes, I guess that is the main reason I restrict. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not every single time, no… or at least I don’t think so. If it’s something that feels “safe” to me, I can sometimes just eat it and not have it take over my whole head.

But if it’s something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich or something that feels really dense, then yeah, it comes up almost immediately. It’s like I’m not even just thinking about the food anymore—I’m already imagining what it’s going to do to me. I get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up the next morning bigger, heavier, slower… like my body won’t move right.

And I know that sounds dramatic, because logically I know one meal isn’t supposed to transform you overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel logical. I start picturing myself at work trying to get around ladders and through rooms, bending and reaching and carrying things, and I just imagine feeling bulky or clumsy, like I won’t fit through the space the same way or won’t be able to move how I need to.

So… not every time I eat, exactly. But any time the food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavy, then yes, it’s right there.

People talk about food like it’s this big enjoyable thing, and for me it’s just… I don’t know, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess, yeah, a little. I don’t really weigh myself all the time or anything, so I don’t have some exact number to give you, but I’ve noticed my work pants are definitely looser than they used to be. Like, the ones I wear on job sites, they kind of hang on me now, and I’m always pulling them up or tightening my belt more than I remember having to.\n\nAnd people have been commenting, which is honestly kind of irritating. Like coworkers or people on jobs will say, “You look too thin,” or ask if I’m eating enough, and I don’t really know what they want me to say to that. I feel like I’m just on my feet all day, climbing ladders, carrying stuff, moving around, so to me it makes sense that I’ve just… leaned out some. It doesn’t feel like this big dramatic thing the way other people make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess I am. Not like I sit around thinking about numbers all day, but if I eat something that feels heavy—like pizza, or a really big sandwich, something like that—I get this really uncomfortable panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s like my brain goes straight to, okay, tomorrow I’m going to wake up and feel bigger, heavier, slower. And I know that probably sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels really real. I start thinking about work, like being up on ladders or squeezing around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans and trying to move quickly, and I just picture myself not being able to move the way I need to. Like I’ll be sluggish or clumsy or just… too much in my own body.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult about food. It’s more that certain foods make me feel like I’m going to lose control of how I feel in my body, and then I get tense about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when deciding what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, probably more than I want to admit. It’s not always like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but when I’m deciding what to eat, I definitely think about how it’s going to make me feel in my body.\n\nLike if it’s something heavier—pizza, or a big sandwich, or something really filling—I’ll hesitate. I start thinking, if I eat that, am I going to feel bigger afterward? Am I going to wake up tomorrow feeling heavy or slow? And then it sort of spirals from there, because my work is so physical. I’m on ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit around furniture and drop cloths and paint cans, and I hate the idea of feeling like my body is in the way.\n\nSo yeah, weight is part of it. Maybe I don’t always call it that in my head, but that’s what it is. I’ll choose something lighter because it feels safer, like I’ll still be able to move the way I need to. With heavier food, I get this panicky feeling, like one meal is going to somehow make me bigger and slower, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “I’m skipping food because I’m scared of gaining weight,” because that sounds really blunt, but that’s kind of what it comes down to.\n\nIf the only thing around is something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I’ll put it off or just not have it. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ll say I’ll eat later, but really it’s because I get this panicky feeling like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Bigger and slower. And then I start thinking about work, being on ladders, moving through rooms, bending around furniture and buckets and drop cloths, and I just picture myself feeling clumsy or heavy in my body.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t change everything overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like just one meal. It feels like I’m risking losing the way I can move, if that makes sense. So yeah, I do avoid eating sometimes when food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavier.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because I’ll dress it up as, like, “I just don’t want to feel sluggish,” or “I need to be able to move for work,” but underneath that, yeah, it’s the fear of gaining weight or feeling bigger.\n\nLike if I’m looking at something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something really filling—I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a lot of food.” I get this panicky feeling, like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up the next day bigger and slower somehow. And then I picture myself at work, trying to get around ladders and drop cloths and furniture, or squeezing through rooms with paint cans and all my stuff, and I feel like my body won’t move the way I need it to.\n\nSo yes, I guess that is the main reason I restrict. It’s not that I’m trying to make some big statement about food. It’s more like I’m trying to avoid that panic, that feeling that I’m going to become heavier and not be able to function right in my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight now?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I look overweight now. I mean, I know other people act like my weight is this huge, urgent problem, but when I look at myself, that’s not what I see.\n\nTo me it feels like people are making a really big deal out of something that’s more about me being disciplined, or being busy, or just keeping myself able to work the way I need to. I’m on my feet all day, I’m moving around, and I don’t want to feel weighed down. So when someone says I’m too thin or they act alarmed, it’s hard for me to really take that in, because in my head it doesn’t feel like some emergency.\n\nI’m not saying I think I’m overweight exactly. It’s more like… I don’t see what everyone else seems to be so worried about. I feel like they’re looking at me and deciding it’s worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the possibility of gaining weight every time you eat?\n\nPatient: Not every single time, no… or at least I don’t think so. If it’s something that feels “safe” to me, I can sometimes just eat it and not have it take over my whole head.\n\nBut if it’s something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich or something that feels really dense, then yeah, it comes up almost immediately. It’s like I’m not even just thinking about the food anymore—I’m already imagining what it’s going to do to me. I get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up the next morning bigger, heavier, slower… like my body won’t move right.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic, because logically I know one meal isn’t supposed to transform you overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel logical. I start picturing myself at work trying to get around ladders and through rooms, bending and reaching and carrying things, and I just imagine feeling bulky or clumsy, like I won’t fit through the space the same way or won’t be able to move how I need to.\n\nSo… not every time I eat, exactly. But any time the food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavy, then yes, it’s right there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F50.0", "F50.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

But any time the food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavy, then yes, it’s right there.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you look fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / painter_or_paperhanger / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition\nmanifestation: \"I don't really think my weight is some emergency like everyone keeps acting; to me it feels like they're making a big deal out of me being disciplined and busy.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d say I look fat, exactly. That word feels… too simple for what happens in my head. It’s more like when other people say I’m too thin, I don’t really see it the way they do. I hear them, but it feels like they’re reacting to something much more dramatic than what I’m actually seeing or feeling.

Like, to me, it doesn’t feel like my weight is this emergency everyone keeps acting like it is. It feels more like I’m just being disciplined, or I’m busy, or I’m trying to keep myself able to function and move around for work. And then when someone looks worried or says I need to gain weight, it almost feels like they’re misunderstanding it, like they’re making a huge deal out of something that, in my mind, is just me managing things.

So… no, I don’t think I look fat when they say I’m too thin. But I also don’t fully believe them, I guess. I don’t look at myself and think, “This is dangerous,” the way they seem to.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… honestly, I’m here because the doctor at the clinic kind of got on me about my eating. I didn’t really think it was something I needed a whole appointment for, but they were pretty insistent.\n\nMost days I just keep things simple, you know? Like I’ll have coffee in the morning, especially if I’m heading to a job early, and then maybe I’ll grab a few bites of something between painting jobs if there’s time or if I remember. And then dinner is usually small, if I’m not too busy with class paperwork or too tired by the time I get home. It’s not like I’m trying to make it complicated—I just sort of have a routine, and food ends up being… not the main thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy eating when you do have the time for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “enjoy” it exactly. I mean, sometimes if something tastes good, sure, I can recognize that it tastes good. Like if someone made something really nice or if there’s a specific thing I’m in the mood for, I’m not saying I’m incapable of liking flavors or anything.\n\nBut most of the time eating feels more like… a task? Like something I have to fit in around everything else. And sometimes by the time I sit down, I’m already thinking about what I still need to do, or whether I have enough time before I have to prep for the next job or finish paperwork, so it’s hard to really relax and enjoy it. I guess I don’t pay attention to it the way other people seem to. People talk about food like it’s this big enjoyable thing, and for me it’s just… I don’t know, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess, yeah, a little. I don’t really weigh myself all the time or anything, so I don’t have some exact number to give you, but I’ve noticed my work pants are definitely looser than they used to be. Like, the ones I wear on job sites, they kind of hang on me now, and I’m always pulling them up or tightening my belt more than I remember having to.\n\nAnd people have been commenting, which is honestly kind of irritating. Like coworkers or people on jobs will say, “You look too thin,” or ask if I’m eating enough, and I don’t really know what they want me to say to that. I feel like I’m just on my feet all day, climbing ladders, carrying stuff, moving around, so to me it makes sense that I’ve just… leaned out some. It doesn’t feel like this big dramatic thing the way other people make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess I am. Not like I sit around thinking about numbers all day, but if I eat something that feels heavy—like pizza, or a really big sandwich, something like that—I get this really uncomfortable panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s like my brain goes straight to, okay, tomorrow I’m going to wake up and feel bigger, heavier, slower. And I know that probably sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels really real. I start thinking about work, like being up on ladders or squeezing around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans and trying to move quickly, and I just picture myself not being able to move the way I need to. Like I’ll be sluggish or clumsy or just… too much in my own body.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult about food. It’s more that certain foods make me feel like I’m going to lose control of how I feel in my body, and then I get tense about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when deciding what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, probably more than I want to admit. It’s not always like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but when I’m deciding what to eat, I definitely think about how it’s going to make me feel in my body.\n\nLike if it’s something heavier—pizza, or a big sandwich, or something really filling—I’ll hesitate. I start thinking, if I eat that, am I going to feel bigger afterward? Am I going to wake up tomorrow feeling heavy or slow? And then it sort of spirals from there, because my work is so physical. I’m on ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit around furniture and drop cloths and paint cans, and I hate the idea of feeling like my body is in the way.\n\nSo yeah, weight is part of it. Maybe I don’t always call it that in my head, but that’s what it is. I’ll choose something lighter because it feels safer, like I’ll still be able to move the way I need to. With heavier food, I get this panicky feeling, like one meal is going to somehow make me bigger and slower, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “I’m skipping food because I’m scared of gaining weight,” because that sounds really blunt, but that’s kind of what it comes down to.\n\nIf the only thing around is something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I’ll put it off or just not have it. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ll say I’ll eat later, but really it’s because I get this panicky feeling like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Bigger and slower. And then I start thinking about work, being on ladders, moving through rooms, bending around furniture and buckets and drop cloths, and I just picture myself feeling clumsy or heavy in my body.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t change everything overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like just one meal. It feels like I’m risking losing the way I can move, if that makes sense. So yeah, I do avoid eating sometimes when food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavier.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because I’ll dress it up as, like, “I just don’t want to feel sluggish,” or “I need to be able to move for work,” but underneath that, yeah, it’s the fear of gaining weight or feeling bigger.\n\nLike if I’m looking at something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something really filling—I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a lot of food.” I get this panicky feeling, like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up the next day bigger and slower somehow. And then I picture myself at work, trying to get around ladders and drop cloths and furniture, or squeezing through rooms with paint cans and all my stuff, and I feel like my body won’t move the way I need it to.\n\nSo yes, I guess that is the main reason I restrict. It’s not that I’m trying to make some big statement about food. It’s more like I’m trying to avoid that panic, that feeling that I’m going to become heavier and not be able to function right in my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight now?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I look overweight now. I mean, I know other people act like my weight is this huge, urgent problem, but when I look at myself, that’s not what I see.\n\nTo me it feels like people are making a really big deal out of something that’s more about me being disciplined, or being busy, or just keeping myself able to work the way I need to. I’m on my feet all day, I’m moving around, and I don’t want to feel weighed down. So when someone says I’m too thin or they act alarmed, it’s hard for me to really take that in, because in my head it doesn’t feel like some emergency.\n\nI’m not saying I think I’m overweight exactly. It’s more like… I don’t see what everyone else seems to be so worried about. I feel like they’re looking at me and deciding it’s worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the possibility of gaining weight every time you eat?\n\nPatient: Not every single time, no… or at least I don’t think so. If it’s something that feels “safe” to me, I can sometimes just eat it and not have it take over my whole head.\n\nBut if it’s something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich or something that feels really dense, then yeah, it comes up almost immediately. It’s like I’m not even just thinking about the food anymore—I’m already imagining what it’s going to do to me. I get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up the next morning bigger, heavier, slower… like my body won’t move right.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic, because logically I know one meal isn’t supposed to transform you overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel logical. I start picturing myself at work trying to get around ladders and through rooms, bending and reaching and carrying things, and I just imagine feeling bulky or clumsy, like I won’t fit through the space the same way or won’t be able to move how I need to.\n\nSo… not every time I eat, exactly. But any time the food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavy, then yes, it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I look fat, exactly. That word feels… too simple for what happens in my head. It’s more like when other people say I’m too thin, I don’t really see it the way they do. I hear them, but it feels like they’re reacting to something much more dramatic than what I’m actually seeing or feeling.\n\nLike, to me, it doesn’t feel like my weight is this emergency everyone keeps acting like it is. It feels more like I’m just being disciplined, or I’m busy, or I’m trying to keep myself able to function and move around for work. And then when someone looks worried or says I need to gain weight, it almost feels like they’re misunderstanding it, like they’re making a huge deal out of something that, in my mind, is just me managing things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I look fat when they say I’m too thin. But I also don’t fully believe them, I guess. I don’t look at myself and think, “This is dangerous,” the way they seem to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

If it’s something that feels “safe” to me, I can sometimes just eat it and not have it take over my whole head.\n\nBut if it’s something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich or something that feels really dense, then yeah, it comes up almost immediately. It’s like I’m not even just thinking about the food anymore—I’m already imagining what it’s going to do to me. I get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up the next morning bigger, heavier, slower… like my body won’t move right.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic, because logically I know one meal isn’t supposed to transform you overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel logical. I start picturing myself at work trying to get around ladders and through rooms, bending and reaching and carrying things, and I just imagine feeling bulky or clumsy, like I won’t fit through the space the same way or won’t be able to move how I need to.\n\nSo… not every time I eat, exactly. But any time the food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavy, then yes, it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I look fat, exactly. That word feels… too simple for what happens in my head. It’s more like when other people say I’m too thin, I don’t really see it the way they do. I hear them, but it feels like they’re reacting to something much more dramatic than what I’m actually seeing or feeling.\n\nLike, to me, it doesn’t feel like my weight is this emergency everyone keeps acting like it is. It feels more like I’m just being disciplined, or I’m busy, or I’m trying to keep myself able to function and move around for work. And then when someone looks worried or says I need to gain weight, it almost feels like they’re misunderstanding it, like they’re making a huge deal out of something that, in my mind, is just me managing things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I look fat when they say I’m too thin. But I also don’t fully believe them, I guess. I don’t look at myself and think, “This is dangerous,” the way they seem to.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
When I look in the mirror, I don’t really think, “Oh, I look underweight,” no. I mean… I can tell I’m not big, obviously. I’m not saying I look the same as everyone else or anything like that. But I don’t see it as this alarming thing people keep describing.

It’s more like—I look at myself and I think, okay, I’m keeping myself in control, I’m staying disciplined, I’m managing with how busy I am and how physical my work can be. I’m moving around all day, and I don’t want to feel weighed down or like my body is getting in the way. So when other people act like my weight is some emergency, it doesn’t match what I’m seeing.

I guess I can understand that maybe I look thinner than some people expect, but “underweight” sounds so serious. It sounds like something dangerous, and that’s not really how it feels to me when I look in the mirror. It feels like everyone else is making a big deal out of it.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… honestly, I’m here because the doctor at the clinic kind of got on me about my eating. I didn’t really think it was something I needed a whole appointment for, but they were pretty insistent.\n\nMost days I just keep things simple, you know? Like I’ll have coffee in the morning, especially if I’m heading to a job early, and then maybe I’ll grab a few bites of something between painting jobs if there’s time or if I remember. And then dinner is usually small, if I’m not too busy with class paperwork or too tired by the time I get home. It’s not like I’m trying to make it complicated—I just sort of have a routine, and food ends up being… not the main thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy eating when you do have the time for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “enjoy” it exactly. I mean, sometimes if something tastes good, sure, I can recognize that it tastes good. Like if someone made something really nice or if there’s a specific thing I’m in the mood for, I’m not saying I’m incapable of liking flavors or anything.\n\nBut most of the time eating feels more like… a task? Like something I have to fit in around everything else. And sometimes by the time I sit down, I’m already thinking about what I still need to do, or whether I have enough time before I have to prep for the next job or finish paperwork, so it’s hard to really relax and enjoy it. I guess I don’t pay attention to it the way other people seem to. People talk about food like it’s this big enjoyable thing, and for me it’s just… I don’t know, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess, yeah, a little. I don’t really weigh myself all the time or anything, so I don’t have some exact number to give you, but I’ve noticed my work pants are definitely looser than they used to be. Like, the ones I wear on job sites, they kind of hang on me now, and I’m always pulling them up or tightening my belt more than I remember having to.\n\nAnd people have been commenting, which is honestly kind of irritating. Like coworkers or people on jobs will say, “You look too thin,” or ask if I’m eating enough, and I don’t really know what they want me to say to that. I feel like I’m just on my feet all day, climbing ladders, carrying stuff, moving around, so to me it makes sense that I’ve just… leaned out some. It doesn’t feel like this big dramatic thing the way other people make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess I am. Not like I sit around thinking about numbers all day, but if I eat something that feels heavy—like pizza, or a really big sandwich, something like that—I get this really uncomfortable panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s like my brain goes straight to, okay, tomorrow I’m going to wake up and feel bigger, heavier, slower. And I know that probably sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels really real. I start thinking about work, like being up on ladders or squeezing around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans and trying to move quickly, and I just picture myself not being able to move the way I need to. Like I’ll be sluggish or clumsy or just… too much in my own body.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult about food. It’s more that certain foods make me feel like I’m going to lose control of how I feel in my body, and then I get tense about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when deciding what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, probably more than I want to admit. It’s not always like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but when I’m deciding what to eat, I definitely think about how it’s going to make me feel in my body.\n\nLike if it’s something heavier—pizza, or a big sandwich, or something really filling—I’ll hesitate. I start thinking, if I eat that, am I going to feel bigger afterward? Am I going to wake up tomorrow feeling heavy or slow? And then it sort of spirals from there, because my work is so physical. I’m on ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit around furniture and drop cloths and paint cans, and I hate the idea of feeling like my body is in the way.\n\nSo yeah, weight is part of it. Maybe I don’t always call it that in my head, but that’s what it is. I’ll choose something lighter because it feels safer, like I’ll still be able to move the way I need to. With heavier food, I get this panicky feeling, like one meal is going to somehow make me bigger and slower, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “I’m skipping food because I’m scared of gaining weight,” because that sounds really blunt, but that’s kind of what it comes down to.\n\nIf the only thing around is something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I’ll put it off or just not have it. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ll say I’ll eat later, but really it’s because I get this panicky feeling like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Bigger and slower. And then I start thinking about work, being on ladders, moving through rooms, bending around furniture and buckets and drop cloths, and I just picture myself feeling clumsy or heavy in my body.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t change everything overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like just one meal. It feels like I’m risking losing the way I can move, if that makes sense. So yeah, I do avoid eating sometimes when food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavier.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because I’ll dress it up as, like, “I just don’t want to feel sluggish,” or “I need to be able to move for work,” but underneath that, yeah, it’s the fear of gaining weight or feeling bigger.\n\nLike if I’m looking at something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something really filling—I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a lot of food.” I get this panicky feeling, like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up the next day bigger and slower somehow. And then I picture myself at work, trying to get around ladders and drop cloths and furniture, or squeezing through rooms with paint cans and all my stuff, and I feel like my body won’t move the way I need it to.\n\nSo yes, I guess that is the main reason I restrict. It’s not that I’m trying to make some big statement about food. It’s more like I’m trying to avoid that panic, that feeling that I’m going to become heavier and not be able to function right in my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight now?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I look overweight now. I mean, I know other people act like my weight is this huge, urgent problem, but when I look at myself, that’s not what I see.\n\nTo me it feels like people are making a really big deal out of something that’s more about me being disciplined, or being busy, or just keeping myself able to work the way I need to. I’m on my feet all day, I’m moving around, and I don’t want to feel weighed down. So when someone says I’m too thin or they act alarmed, it’s hard for me to really take that in, because in my head it doesn’t feel like some emergency.\n\nI’m not saying I think I’m overweight exactly. It’s more like… I don’t see what everyone else seems to be so worried about. I feel like they’re looking at me and deciding it’s worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the possibility of gaining weight every time you eat?\n\nPatient: Not every single time, no… or at least I don’t think so. If it’s something that feels “safe” to me, I can sometimes just eat it and not have it take over my whole head.\n\nBut if it’s something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich or something that feels really dense, then yeah, it comes up almost immediately. It’s like I’m not even just thinking about the food anymore—I’m already imagining what it’s going to do to me. I get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up the next morning bigger, heavier, slower… like my body won’t move right.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic, because logically I know one meal isn’t supposed to transform you overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel logical. I start picturing myself at work trying to get around ladders and through rooms, bending and reaching and carrying things, and I just imagine feeling bulky or clumsy, like I won’t fit through the space the same way or won’t be able to move how I need to.\n\nSo… not every time I eat, exactly. But any time the food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavy, then yes, it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I look fat, exactly. That word feels… too simple for what happens in my head. It’s more like when other people say I’m too thin, I don’t really see it the way they do. I hear them, but it feels like they’re reacting to something much more dramatic than what I’m actually seeing or feeling.\n\nLike, to me, it doesn’t feel like my weight is this emergency everyone keeps acting like it is. And then when someone looks worried or says I need to gain weight, it almost feels like they’re misunderstanding it, like they’re making a huge deal out of something that, in my mind, is just me managing things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I look fat when they say I’m too thin. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because I’ll dress it up as, like, “I just don’t want to feel sluggish,” or “I need to be able to move for work,” but underneath that, yeah, it’s the fear of gaining weight or feeling bigger.\n\nLike if I’m looking at something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something really filling—I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a lot of food.” I get this panicky feeling, like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up the next day bigger and slower somehow. And then I picture myself at work, trying to get around ladders and drop cloths and furniture, or squeezing through rooms with paint cans and all my stuff, and I feel like my body won’t move the way I need it to.\n\nSo yes, I guess that is the main reason I restrict. It’s not that I’m trying to make some big statement about food. It’s more like I’m trying to avoid that panic, that feeling that I’m going to become heavier and not be able to function right in my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight now?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I look overweight now. I mean, I know other people act like my weight is this huge, urgent problem, but when I look at myself, that’s not what I see.\n\nTo me it feels like people are making a really big deal out of something that’s more about me being disciplined, or being busy, or just keeping myself able to work the way I need to. I’m on my feet all day, I’m moving around, and I don’t want to feel weighed down. So when someone says I’m too thin or they act alarmed, it’s hard for me to really take that in, because in my head it doesn’t feel like some emergency.\n\nI’m not saying I think I’m overweight exactly. It’s more like… I don’t see what everyone else seems to be so worried about. I feel like they’re looking at me and deciding it’s worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the possibility of gaining weight every time you eat?\n\nPatient: Not every single time, no… or at least I don’t think so. If it’s something that feels “safe” to me, I can sometimes just eat it and not have it take over my whole head.\n\nBut if it’s something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich or something that feels really dense, then yeah, it comes up almost immediately. It’s like I’m not even just thinking about the food anymore—I’m already imagining what it’s going to do to me. I get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up the next morning bigger, heavier, slower… like my body won’t move right.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic, because logically I know one meal isn’t supposed to transform you overnight. But any time the food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavy, then yes, it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I look fat, exactly. I’m moving around all day, and I don’t want to feel weighed down or like my body is getting in the way. I guess… not exactly. I mean, I know other people are seeing the same body, technically, but I don’t think they’re interpreting it the same way I am.

When they look at me, it feels like they jump straight to concern, like my weight is some huge emergency or something that needs to be fixed right away. So when people act shocked or worried, I kind of feel like… you’re making this into a bigger thing than it is.

I’m not saying they’re lying, exactly. I know they must see something that bothers them. But I don’t see it with that same urgency. To me it feels like they’re reacting to me being disciplined, or to me not eating the way they think I should, and turning it into this whole dramatic thing. So I guess my weight probably isn’t the same in my head as it is to them, no.

It’s not like I’m trying to make it complicated—I just sort of have a routine, and food ends up being… not the main thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy eating when you do have the time for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “enjoy” it exactly. I feel like I’m just on my feet all day, climbing ladders, carrying stuff, moving around, so to me it makes sense that I’ve just… leaned out some. It doesn’t feel like this big dramatic thing the way other people make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess I am. It’s more that certain foods make me feel like I’m going to lose control of how I feel in my body, and then I get tense about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when deciding what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, probably more than I want to admit. It’s not always like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but when I’m deciding what to eat, I definitely think about how it’s going to make me feel in my body.\n\nLike if it’s something heavier—pizza, or a big sandwich, or something really filling—I’ll hesitate. I start thinking, if I eat that, am I going to feel bigger afterward? Am I going to wake up tomorrow feeling heavy or slow? And then it sort of spirals from there, because my work is so physical. I’m on ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit around furniture and drop cloths and paint cans, and I hate the idea of feeling like my body is in the way.\n\nSo yeah, weight is part of it. Maybe I don’t always call it that in my head, but that’s what it is. I’ll choose something lighter because it feels safer, like I’ll still be able to move the way I need to. With heavier food, I get this panicky feeling, like one meal is going to somehow make me bigger and slower, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “I’m skipping food because I’m scared of gaining weight,” because that sounds really blunt, but that’s kind of what it comes down to.\n\nIf the only thing around is something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I’ll put it off or just not have it. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ll say I’ll eat later, but really it’s because I get this panicky feeling like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Bigger and slower. And then I start thinking about work, being on ladders, moving through rooms, bending around furniture and buckets and drop cloths, and I just picture myself feeling clumsy or heavy in my body.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t change everything overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like just one meal. It feels like I’m risking losing the way I can move, if that makes sense. So yeah, I do avoid eating sometimes when food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavier.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because I’ll dress it up as, like, “I just don’t want to feel sluggish,” or “I need to be able to move for work,” but underneath that, yeah, it’s the fear of gaining weight or feeling bigger.\n\nLike if I’m looking at something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something really filling—I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a lot of food.” I get this panicky feeling, like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up the next day bigger and slower somehow. And then I picture myself at work, trying to get around ladders and drop cloths and furniture, or squeezing through rooms with paint cans and all my stuff, and I feel like my body won’t move the way I need it to.\n\nSo yes, I guess that is the main reason I restrict. It’s not that I’m trying to make some big statement about food. It’s more like I’m trying to avoid that panic, that feeling that I’m going to become heavier and not be able to function right in my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight now?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I look overweight now. I’m on my feet all day, I’m moving around, and I don’t want to feel weighed down. So when someone says I’m too thin or they act alarmed, it’s hard for me to really take that in, because in my head it doesn’t feel like some emergency.\n\nI’m not saying I think I’m overweight exactly. It’s more like… I don’t see what everyone else seems to be so worried about. I feel like they’re looking at me and deciding it’s worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the possibility of gaining weight every time you eat?\n\nPatient: Not every single time, no… or at least I don’t think so. If it’s something that feels “safe” to me, I can sometimes just eat it and not have it take over my whole head.\n\nBut if it’s something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich or something that feels really dense, then yeah, it comes up almost immediately. It’s like I’m not even just thinking about the food anymore—I’m already imagining what it’s going to do to me. I get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up the next morning bigger, heavier, slower… like my body won’t move right.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic, because logically I know one meal isn’t supposed to transform you overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel logical. I start picturing myself at work trying to get around ladders and through rooms, bending and reaching and carrying things, and I just imagine feeling bulky or clumsy, like I won’t fit through the space the same way or won’t be able to move how I need to.\n\nSo… not every time I eat, exactly. But any time the food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavy, then yes, it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I look fat, exactly. That word feels… too simple for what happens in my head. It’s more like when other people say I’m too thin, I don’t really see it the way they do. I hear them, but it feels like they’re reacting to something much more dramatic than what I’m actually seeing or feeling.\n\nLike, to me, it doesn’t feel like my weight is this emergency everyone keeps acting like it is. I don’t look at myself and think, “This is dangerous,” the way they seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don’t really think, “Oh, I look underweight,” no. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it is, but not in the way people probably mean when they ask that. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking I want to look like some specific picture or be a certain clothing size. It’s more tied to how I imagine my body working, I guess.

Like if I eat something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something that feels really dense—I get this immediate panic that it’s going to change me overnight. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’ll wake up and my body will be bigger, slower, harder to move. And then I start thinking about work, because I’m on ladders, I’m moving around rooms, reaching, bending, carrying things, trying to get into corners. I can’t feel bulky or weighed down doing that. I need to feel light enough and in control enough to move the way I need to.

So I guess the fear is related to how I want my body to look, but it’s also about how I want it to feel. I don’t want to feel like my body is taking up more space or getting in the way. When I imagine gaining weight, I don’t just picture looking different—I picture being slower, less capable, like everything would be harder. That’s what scares me.
