Like if someone made something really nice or if there’s a specific thing I’m in the mood for, I’m not saying I’m incapable of liking flavors or anything.\n\nBut most of the time eating feels more like… a task? Like something I have to fit in around everything else. And sometimes by the time I sit down, I’m already thinking about what I still need to do, or whether I have enough time before I have to prep for the next job or finish paperwork, so it’s hard to really relax and enjoy it. I guess I don’t pay attention to it the way other people seem to. People talk about food like it’s this big enjoyable thing, and for me it’s just… I don’t know, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess, yeah, a little. I don’t really weigh myself all the time or anything, so I don’t have some exact number to give you, but I’ve noticed my work pants are definitely looser than they used to be. Like, the ones I wear on job sites, they kind of hang on me now, and I’m always pulling them up or tightening my belt more than I remember having to.\n\nAnd people have been commenting, which is honestly kind of irritating. Like coworkers or people on jobs will say, “You look too thin,” or ask if I’m eating enough, and I don’t really know what they want me to say to that. I feel like I’m just on my feet all day, climbing ladders, carrying stuff, moving around, so to me it makes sense that I’ve just… leaned out some. It doesn’t feel like this big dramatic thing the way other people make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess I am. Not like I sit around thinking about numbers all day, but if I eat something that feels heavy—like pizza, or a really big sandwich, something like that—I get this really uncomfortable panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s like my brain goes straight to, okay, tomorrow I’m going to wake up and feel bigger, heavier, slower. And I know that probably sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels really real. I start thinking about work, like being up on ladders or squeezing around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans and trying to move quickly, and I just picture myself not being able to move the way I need to. Like I’ll be sluggish or clumsy or just… too much in my own body.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult about food. It’s more that certain foods make me feel like I’m going to lose control of how I feel in my body, and then I get tense about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when deciding what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, probably more than I want to admit. It’s not always like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but when I’m deciding what to eat, I definitely think about how it’s going to make me feel in my body.\n\nLike if it’s something heavier—pizza, or a big sandwich, or something really filling—I’ll hesitate. I start thinking, if I eat that, am I going to feel bigger afterward? Am I going to wake up tomorrow feeling heavy or slow? And then it sort of spirals from there, because my work is so physical. I’m on ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit around furniture and drop cloths and paint cans, and I hate the idea of feeling like my body is in the way.\n\nSo yeah, weight is part of it. Maybe I don’t always call it that in my head, but that’s what it is. I’ll choose something lighter because it feels safer, like I’ll still be able to move the way I need to. With heavier food, I get this panicky feeling, like one meal is going to somehow make me bigger and slower, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “I’m skipping food because I’m scared of gaining weight,” because that sounds really blunt, but that’s kind of what it comes down to.\n\nIf the only thing around is something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I’ll put it off or just not have it. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ll say I’ll eat later, but really it’s because I get this panicky feeling like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Bigger and slower. And then I start thinking about work, being on ladders, moving through rooms, bending around furniture and buckets and drop cloths, and I just picture myself feeling clumsy or heavy in my body.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t change everything overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like just one meal. It feels like I’m risking losing the way I can move, if that makes sense. So yeah, I do avoid eating sometimes when food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavier.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because I’ll dress it up as, like, “I just don’t want to feel sluggish,” or “I need to be able to move for work,” but underneath that, yeah, it’s the fear of gaining weight or feeling bigger.\n\nLike if I’m looking at something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something really filling—I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a lot of food.” I get this panicky feeling, like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up the next day bigger and slower somehow. And then I picture myself at work, trying to get around ladders and drop cloths and furniture, or squeezing through rooms with paint cans and all my stuff, and I feel like my body won’t move the way I need it to.\n\nSo yes, I guess that is the main reason I restrict. It’s not that I’m trying to make some big statement about food. It’s more like I’m trying to avoid that panic, that feeling that I’m going to become heavier and not be able to function right in my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight now?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I look overweight now. I mean, I know other people act like my weight is this huge, urgent problem, but when I look at myself, that’s not what I see.\n\nTo me it feels like people are making a really big deal out of something that’s more about me being disciplined, or being busy, or just keeping myself able to work the way I need to. I’m on my feet all day, I’m moving around, and I don’t want to feel weighed down. So when someone says I’m too thin or they act alarmed, it’s hard for me to really take that in, because in my head it doesn’t feel like some emergency.\n\nI’m not saying I think I’m overweight exactly. It’s more like… I don’t see what everyone else seems to be so worried about. I feel like they’re looking at me and deciding it’s worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the possibility of gaining weight every time you eat?\n\nPatient: Not every single time, no… or at least I don’t think so. If it’s something that feels “safe” to me, I can sometimes just eat it and not have it take over my whole head.\n\nBut if it’s something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich or something that feels really dense, then yeah, it comes up almost immediately. It’s like I’m not even just thinking about the food anymore—I’m already imagining what it’s going to do to me. I get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up the next morning bigger, heavier, slower… like my body won’t move right.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic, because logically I know one meal isn’t supposed to transform you overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel logical. I start picturing myself at work trying to get around ladders and through rooms, bending and reaching and carrying things, and I just imagine feeling bulky or clumsy, like I won’t fit through the space the same way or won’t be able to move how I need to.\n\nSo… not every time I eat, exactly. But any time the food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavy, then yes, it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I look fat, exactly. That word feels… too simple for what happens in my head. It’s more like when other people say I’m too thin, I don’t really see it the way they do. I hear them, but it feels like they’re reacting to something much more dramatic than what I’m actually seeing or feeling.\n\nLike, to me, it doesn’t feel like my weight is this emergency everyone keeps acting like it is. It feels more like I’m just being disciplined, or I’m busy, or I’m trying to keep myself able to function and move around for work. And then when someone looks worried or says I need to gain weight, it almost feels like they’re misunderstanding it, like they’re making a huge deal out of something that, in my mind, is just me managing things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I look fat when they say I’m too thin. But I also don’t fully believe them, I guess. I don’t look at myself and think, “This is dangerous,” the way they seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don’t really think, “Oh, I look underweight,” no. I mean… I can tell I’m not big, obviously. I’m not saying I look the same as everyone else or anything like that. But I don’t see it as this alarming thing people keep describing.\n\nIt’s more like—I look at myself and I think, okay, I’m keeping myself in control, I’m staying disciplined, I’m managing with how busy I am and how physical my work can be. I’m moving around all day, and I don’t want to feel weighed down or like my body is getting in the way. So when other people act like my weight is some emergency, it doesn’t match what I’m seeing.\n\nI guess I can understand that maybe I look thinner than some people expect, but “underweight” sounds so serious. It sounds like something dangerous, and that’s not really how it feels to me when I look in the mirror. It feels like everyone else is making a big deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what other people see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I guess… not exactly. I mean, I know other people are seeing the same body, technically, but I don’t think they’re interpreting it the same way I am.\n\nWhen they look at me, it feels like they jump straight to concern, like my weight is some huge emergency or something that needs to be fixed right away. And when I look at myself, or when I think about my weight, it doesn’t land that way for me. It feels more like I’m just keeping things controlled. Like I’m busy, I’m working, I’m on my feet, I need to stay able to move and not feel weighed down. So when people act shocked or worried, I kind of feel like… you’re making this into a bigger thing than it is.\n\nI’m not saying they’re lying, exactly. I know they must see something that bothers them. But I don’t see it with that same urgency. To me it feels like they’re reacting to me being disciplined, or to me not eating the way they think I should, and turning it into this whole dramatic thing. So I guess my weight probably isn’t the same in my head as it is to them, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you want your body to look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, but not in the way people probably mean when they ask that. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking I want to look like some specific picture or be a certain clothing size. It’s more tied to how I imagine my body working, I guess.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something that feels really dense—I get this immediate panic that it’s going to change me overnight. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’ll wake up and my body will be bigger, slower, harder to move. And then I start thinking about work, because I’m on ladders, I’m moving around rooms, reaching, bending, carrying things, trying to get into corners. I can’t feel bulky or weighed down doing that. I need to feel light enough and in control enough to move the way I need to.\n\nSo I guess the fear is related to how I want my body to look, but it’s also about how I want it to feel. I don’t want to feel like my body is taking up more space or getting in the way. When I imagine gaining weight, I don’t just picture looking different—I picture being slower, less capable, like everything would be harder. That’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you think your body looks to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least partly. I don’t know if it’s only about how I think I look to other people, but I do think about it. Like, if I eat something heavy—pizza or a big sandwich or something like that—I get this panicky feeling almost immediately, like it’s going to show on me. Like I’m going to wake up bigger somehow, even if I know that probably sounds irrational when I say it out loud.\n\nAnd then I start imagining what that would mean when people see me, like they’d notice I was bigger or that I’d lost control or something. But it’s also not just the looking part. It’s like I picture being heavier and slower, like my body would move differently. With my work, I’m up and down ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit into awkward spaces. I need to feel light and able to move. So when I think about gaining weight, I imagine other people seeing me as bigger, yes, but I also imagine feeling clumsy or weighed down.\n\nSo… yes, it’s connected to how I think I might look to others, but it’s really tangled up with this fear that if I eat the wrong thing, my body will change and I won’t be able to move the way I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you perceive your own body shape or size?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s probably the closest way to say it. It’s very connected to how I perceive my own size, even if I don’t always trust that other people see it the same way.\n\nLike, if I eat something that feels heavy — pizza, or a big sandwich, or something with a lot of bread and cheese — I don’t just think, “Oh, I ate a big meal.” My mind goes straight to this feeling that my body is going to be different. Like I’ll wake up the next morning and somehow be bigger, puffier, slower… like my shape will have changed in a way I can feel and see. I know when I say that out loud it sounds like it shouldn’t happen that fast, but in the moment it feels really convincing.\n\nAnd it’s not just about looking in the mirror, though that’s part of it. It’s also this sense of my body taking up more space than it should. I start imagining myself moving around at work and feeling weighed down. I’m on ladders, I’m reaching up, bending, carrying things, squeezing around furniture or into corners, and I need my body to feel manageable. If I feel even a little heavier or bigger, I start thinking I won’t move right, like I’ll be slower or clumsier or just… not able to do what I need to do.\n\nSo yes, my fear of gaining weight is really tied to how I see my own body shape and size. It’s like I’m constantly trying to keep it from becoming something that feels too big or too heavy for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you think your body should look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if I have some clear idea of how my body “should” look, like a picture in my head exactly, but I do have this feeling that it should stay… light, I guess. Manageable. Not bulky or heavy or like it’s taking up too much space.\n\nWhen I eat something that feels heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, it’s not just that I feel full. I get this really panicky feeling, like I’ve done something that’s going to change my body right away. I’ll start thinking, what if I wake up tomorrow and I’m bigger? What if I feel slower? What if I can’t move the same way?\n\nAnd because of my work, that thought really gets to me. I’m climbing ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, working in corners or tight spots, and I need to feel like my body can do all of that without getting in the way. So when I think about how my body should look, it’s tied up with how I think it should function. Like it should be small enough or light enough that I can move easily and not feel weighed down.\n\nSo yes, the fear of gaining weight is related to that. It’s like I’m scared that if I eat the wrong thing, I’ll wake up in a body that feels too big or too slow for what I need it to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s more tied to how I imagine my body working, I guess.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something that feels really dense—I get this immediate panic that it’s going to change me overnight. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’ll wake up and my body will be bigger, slower, harder to move. And then I start thinking about work, because I’m on ladders, I’m moving around rooms, reaching, bending, carrying things, trying to get into corners. I can’t feel bulky or weighed down doing that. I need to feel light enough and in control enough to move the way I need to.\n\nSo I guess the fear is related to how I want my body to look, but it’s also about how I want it to feel. I don’t want to feel like my body is taking up more space or getting in the way. When I imagine gaining weight, I don’t just picture looking different—I picture being slower, less capable, like everything would be harder. That’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you think your body looks to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least partly. I don’t know if it’s only about how I think I look to other people, but I do think about it. Like, if I eat something heavy—pizza or a big sandwich or something like that—I get this panicky feeling almost immediately, like it’s going to show on me. Like I’m going to wake up bigger somehow, even if I know that probably sounds irrational when I say it out loud.\n\nAnd then I start imagining what that would mean when people see me, like they’d notice I was bigger or that I’d lost control or something. But it’s also not just the looking part. It’s like I picture being heavier and slower, like my body would move differently. With my work, I’m up and down ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit into awkward spaces. I need to feel light and able to move. So when I think about gaining weight, I imagine other people seeing me as bigger, yes, but I also imagine feeling clumsy or weighed down.\n\nSo… yes, it’s connected to how I think I might look to others, but it’s really tangled up with this fear that if I eat the wrong thing, my body will change and I won’t be able to move the way I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you perceive your own body shape or size?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s probably the closest way to say it. It’s very connected to how I perceive my own size, even if I don’t always trust that other people see it the same way.\n\nLike, if I eat something that feels heavy — pizza, or a big sandwich, or something with a lot of bread and cheese — I don’t just think, “Oh, I ate a big meal.” My mind goes straight to this feeling that my body is going to be different. Like I’ll wake up the next morning and somehow be bigger, puffier, slower… like my shape will have changed in a way I can feel and see. I know when I say that out loud it sounds like it shouldn’t happen that fast, but in the moment it feels really convincing.\n\nAnd it’s not just about looking in the mirror, though that’s part of it. It’s also this sense of my body taking up more space than it should. I start imagining myself moving around at work and feeling weighed down. If I feel even a little heavier or bigger, I start thinking I won’t move right, like I’ll be slower or clumsier or just… not able to do what I need to do.\n\nSo yes, my fear of gaining weight is really tied to how I see my own body shape and size. It’s like I’m constantly trying to keep it from becoming something that feels too big or too heavy for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you think your body should look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t think I see myself as, like, wildly different from reality, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I look in the mirror and think I’m a completely different person or anything.

But at the same time, I don’t really know how accurate I am. Some days I’ll look at myself and feel like I’m bigger than I expected, or like certain parts of me are taking up more space, and then later I’m not sure if that was real or just how I was feeling in that moment. It can shift depending on what I’ve eaten or how my clothes feel or even just how tense I am.

So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I see my body as a different size than it actually is,” because I don’t have some outside measurement in my head to compare it to. But I also don’t fully trust my own perception all the time. It can feel very real to me in the moment, even if afterward I can question it.

Like I’ll have coffee in the morning, especially if I’m heading to a job early, and then maybe I’ll grab a few bites of something between painting jobs if there’s time or if I remember. And then dinner is usually small, if I’m not too busy with class paperwork or too tired by the time I get home. It’s not like I’m trying to make it complicated—I just sort of have a routine, and food ends up being… not the main thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy eating when you do have the time for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “enjoy” it exactly. Like if someone made something really nice or if there’s a specific thing I’m in the mood for, I’m not saying I’m incapable of liking flavors or anything.\n\nBut most of the time eating feels more like… a task? Like something I have to fit in around everything else. I guess I don’t pay attention to it the way other people seem to. People talk about food like it’s this big enjoyable thing, and for me it’s just… I don’t know, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess, yeah, a little. I don’t really weigh myself all the time or anything, so I don’t have some exact number to give you, but I’ve noticed my work pants are definitely looser than they used to be. Like, the ones I wear on job sites, they kind of hang on me now, and I’m always pulling them up or tightening my belt more than I remember having to.\n\nAnd people have been commenting, which is honestly kind of irritating. Like coworkers or people on jobs will say, “You look too thin,” or ask if I’m eating enough, and I don’t really know what they want me to say to that. I feel like I’m just on my feet all day, climbing ladders, carrying stuff, moving around, so to me it makes sense that I’ve just… leaned out some. It doesn’t feel like this big dramatic thing the way other people make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess I am. Not like I sit around thinking about numbers all day, but if I eat something that feels heavy—like pizza, or a really big sandwich, something like that—I get this really uncomfortable panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s like my brain goes straight to, okay, tomorrow I’m going to wake up and feel bigger, heavier, slower. And I know that probably sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels really real. I start thinking about work, like being up on ladders or squeezing around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans and trying to move quickly, and I just picture myself not being able to move the way I need to. Like I’ll be sluggish or clumsy or just… too much in my own body.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult about food. It’s more that certain foods make me feel like I’m going to lose control of how I feel in my body, and then I get tense about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when deciding what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, probably more than I want to admit. It’s not always like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but when I’m deciding what to eat, I definitely think about how it’s going to make me feel in my body.\n\nLike if it’s something heavier—pizza, or a big sandwich, or something really filling—I’ll hesitate. I start thinking, if I eat that, am I going to feel bigger afterward? Am I going to wake up tomorrow feeling heavy or slow? And then it sort of spirals from there, because my work is so physical. I’m on ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit around furniture and drop cloths and paint cans, and I hate the idea of feeling like my body is in the way.\n\nSo yeah, weight is part of it. Maybe I don’t always call it that in my head, but that’s what it is. I’ll choose something lighter because it feels safer, like I’ll still be able to move the way I need to. With heavier food, I get this panicky feeling, like one meal is going to somehow make me bigger and slower, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “I’m skipping food because I’m scared of gaining weight,” because that sounds really blunt, but that’s kind of what it comes down to.\n\nIf the only thing around is something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I’ll put it off or just not have it. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ll say I’ll eat later, but really it’s because I get this panicky feeling like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Bigger and slower. And then I start thinking about work, being on ladders, moving through rooms, bending around furniture and buckets and drop cloths, and I just picture myself feeling clumsy or heavy in my body.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t change everything overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like just one meal. It feels like I’m risking losing the way I can move, if that makes sense. So yeah, I do avoid eating sometimes when food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavier.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because I’ll dress it up as, like, “I just don’t want to feel sluggish,” or “I need to be able to move for work,” but underneath that, yeah, it’s the fear of gaining weight or feeling bigger.\n\nLike if I’m looking at something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something really filling—I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a lot of food.” I get this panicky feeling, like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up the next day bigger and slower somehow. And then I picture myself at work, trying to get around ladders and drop cloths and furniture, or squeezing through rooms with paint cans and all my stuff, and I feel like my body won’t move the way I need it to.\n\nSo yes, I guess that is the main reason I restrict. It’s not that I’m trying to make some big statement about food. It’s more like I’m trying to avoid that panic, that feeling that I’m going to become heavier and not be able to function right in my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight now?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I look overweight now. I mean, I know other people act like my weight is this huge, urgent problem, but when I look at myself, that’s not what I see.\n\nTo me it feels like people are making a really big deal out of something that’s more about me being disciplined, or being busy, or just keeping myself able to work the way I need to. I’m on my feet all day, I’m moving around, and I don’t want to feel weighed down. So when someone says I’m too thin or they act alarmed, it’s hard for me to really take that in, because in my head it doesn’t feel like some emergency.\n\nI’m not saying I think I’m overweight exactly. It’s more like… I don’t see what everyone else seems to be so worried about. I feel like they’re looking at me and deciding it’s worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the possibility of gaining weight every time you eat?\n\nPatient: Not every single time, no… or at least I don’t think so. If it’s something that feels “safe” to me, I can sometimes just eat it and not have it take over my whole head.\n\nBut if it’s something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich or something that feels really dense, then yeah, it comes up almost immediately. It’s like I’m not even just thinking about the food anymore—I’m already imagining what it’s going to do to me. I get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up the next morning bigger, heavier, slower… like my body won’t move right.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic, because logically I know one meal isn’t supposed to transform you overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel logical. I start picturing myself at work trying to get around ladders and through rooms, bending and reaching and carrying things, and I just imagine feeling bulky or clumsy, like I won’t fit through the space the same way or won’t be able to move how I need to.\n\nSo… not every time I eat, exactly. But any time the food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavy, then yes, it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I look fat, exactly. That word feels… too simple for what happens in my head. It’s more like when other people say I’m too thin, I don’t really see it the way they do. I hear them, but it feels like they’re reacting to something much more dramatic than what I’m actually seeing or feeling.\n\nLike, to me, it doesn’t feel like my weight is this emergency everyone keeps acting like it is. It feels more like I’m just being disciplined, or I’m busy, or I’m trying to keep myself able to function and move around for work. And then when someone looks worried or says I need to gain weight, it almost feels like they’re misunderstanding it, like they’re making a huge deal out of something that, in my mind, is just me managing things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I look fat when they say I’m too thin. But I also don’t fully believe them, I guess. I don’t look at myself and think, “This is dangerous,” the way they seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don’t really think, “Oh, I look underweight,” no. I mean… I can tell I’m not big, obviously. I’m not saying I look the same as everyone else or anything like that. But I don’t see it as this alarming thing people keep describing.\n\nIt’s more like—I look at myself and I think, okay, I’m keeping myself in control, I’m staying disciplined, I’m managing with how busy I am and how physical my work can be. I’m moving around all day, and I don’t want to feel weighed down or like my body is getting in the way. So when other people act like my weight is some emergency, it doesn’t match what I’m seeing.\n\nI guess I can understand that maybe I look thinner than some people expect, but “underweight” sounds so serious. It sounds like something dangerous, and that’s not really how it feels to me when I look in the mirror. It feels like everyone else is making a big deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what other people see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I guess… not exactly. I mean, I know other people are seeing the same body, technically, but I don’t think they’re interpreting it the same way I am.\n\nWhen they look at me, it feels like they jump straight to concern, like my weight is some huge emergency or something that needs to be fixed right away. And when I look at myself, or when I think about my weight, it doesn’t land that way for me. It feels more like I’m just keeping things controlled. Like I’m busy, I’m working, I’m on my feet, I need to stay able to move and not feel weighed down. So when people act shocked or worried, I kind of feel like… you’re making this into a bigger thing than it is.\n\nI’m not saying they’re lying, exactly. I know they must see something that bothers them. But I don’t see it with that same urgency. To me it feels like they’re reacting to me being disciplined, or to me not eating the way they think I should, and turning it into this whole dramatic thing. So I guess my weight probably isn’t the same in my head as it is to them, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you want your body to look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, but not in the way people probably mean when they ask that. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking I want to look like some specific picture or be a certain clothing size. It’s more tied to how I imagine my body working, I guess.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something that feels really dense—I get this immediate panic that it’s going to change me overnight. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’ll wake up and my body will be bigger, slower, harder to move. And then I start thinking about work, because I’m on ladders, I’m moving around rooms, reaching, bending, carrying things, trying to get into corners. I can’t feel bulky or weighed down doing that. I need to feel light enough and in control enough to move the way I need to.\n\nSo I guess the fear is related to how I want my body to look, but it’s also about how I want it to feel. I don’t want to feel like my body is taking up more space or getting in the way. When I imagine gaining weight, I don’t just picture looking different—I picture being slower, less capable, like everything would be harder. That’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you think your body looks to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least partly. I don’t know if it’s only about how I think I look to other people, but I do think about it. Like, if I eat something heavy—pizza or a big sandwich or something like that—I get this panicky feeling almost immediately, like it’s going to show on me. Like I’m going to wake up bigger somehow, even if I know that probably sounds irrational when I say it out loud.\n\nAnd then I start imagining what that would mean when people see me, like they’d notice I was bigger or that I’d lost control or something. But it’s also not just the looking part. It’s like I picture being heavier and slower, like my body would move differently. With my work, I’m up and down ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit into awkward spaces. I need to feel light and able to move. So when I think about gaining weight, I imagine other people seeing me as bigger, yes, but I also imagine feeling clumsy or weighed down.\n\nSo… yes, it’s connected to how I think I might look to others, but it’s really tangled up with this fear that if I eat the wrong thing, my body will change and I won’t be able to move the way I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you perceive your own body shape or size?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s probably the closest way to say it. It’s very connected to how I perceive my own size, even if I don’t always trust that other people see it the same way.\n\nLike, if I eat something that feels heavy — pizza, or a big sandwich, or something with a lot of bread and cheese — I don’t just think, “Oh, I ate a big meal.” My mind goes straight to this feeling that my body is going to be different. Like I’ll wake up the next morning and somehow be bigger, puffier, slower… like my shape will have changed in a way I can feel and see. I know when I say that out loud it sounds like it shouldn’t happen that fast, but in the moment it feels really convincing.\n\nAnd it’s not just about looking in the mirror, though that’s part of it. It’s also this sense of my body taking up more space than it should. I start imagining myself moving around at work and feeling weighed down. I’m on ladders, I’m reaching up, bending, carrying things, squeezing around furniture or into corners, and I need my body to feel manageable. If I feel even a little heavier or bigger, I start thinking I won’t move right, like I’ll be slower or clumsier or just… not able to do what I need to do.\n\nSo yes, my fear of gaining weight is really tied to how I see my own body shape and size. It’s like I’m constantly trying to keep it from becoming something that feels too big or too heavy for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you think your body should look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if I have some clear idea of how my body “should” look, like a picture in my head exactly, but I do have this feeling that it should stay… light, I guess. Manageable. Not bulky or heavy or like it’s taking up too much space.\n\nWhen I eat something that feels heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, it’s not just that I feel full. I get this really panicky feeling, like I’ve done something that’s going to change my body right away. I’ll start thinking, what if I wake up tomorrow and I’m bigger? What if I feel slower? What if I can’t move the same way?\n\nAnd because of my work, that thought really gets to me. I’m climbing ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, working in corners or tight spots, and I need to feel like my body can do all of that without getting in the way. So when I think about how my body should look, it’s tied up with how I think it should function. Like it should be small enough or light enough that I can move easily and not feel weighed down.\n\nSo yes, the fear of gaining weight is related to that. It’s like I’m scared that if I eat the wrong thing, I’ll wake up in a body that feels too big or too slow for what I need it to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body is a different size or shape than it actually is?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t think I see myself as, like, wildly different from reality, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I look in the mirror and think I’m a completely different person or anything.\n\nBut at the same time, I don’t really know how accurate I am. Some days I’ll look at myself and feel like I’m bigger than I expected, or like certain parts of me are taking up more space, and then later I’m not sure if that was real or just how I was feeling in that moment. It can shift depending on what I’ve eaten or how my clothes feel or even just how tense I am.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I see my body as a different size than it actually is,” because I don’t have some outside measurement in my head to compare it to. But I also don’t fully trust my own perception all the time. It can feel very real to me in the moment, even if afterward I can question it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know. I think that’s the honest answer.

Part of me wants to say yes, because I’m looking at my actual body, you know? It’s not like I’m imagining something out of nowhere. But then another part of me knows that how I see it changes a lot depending on the day, or what I’ve eaten, or how my clothes are sitting, or if I feel full or tense. So if it changes that much, then I guess I can’t be completely sure it’s accurate.

It’s hard because in the moment it feels accurate. Like if I feel bigger, I don’t experience it as “oh, this is just a feeling.” I experience it like it’s information about my body. Then later, sometimes I can step back and think, maybe that wasn’t totally reliable. But I don’t always have that distance when it’s happening.

So… I’m not sure my body image is accurate. I wouldn’t say I trust it completely. I think I treat it like it’s accurate a lot of the time, but I don’t actually know if it is.

And I know that probably sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels really real. I start thinking about work, like being up on ladders or squeezing around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans and trying to move quickly, and I just picture myself not being able to move the way I need to. It’s more that certain foods make me feel like I’m going to lose control of how I feel in my body, and then I get tense about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when deciding what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, probably more than I want to admit. It’s not always like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but when I’m deciding what to eat, I definitely think about how it’s going to make me feel in my body.\n\nLike if it’s something heavier—pizza, or a big sandwich, or something really filling—I’ll hesitate. I start thinking, if I eat that, am I going to feel bigger afterward? Am I going to wake up tomorrow feeling heavy or slow? And then it sort of spirals from there, because my work is so physical. I’m on ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit around furniture and drop cloths and paint cans, and I hate the idea of feeling like my body is in the way.\n\nSo yeah, weight is part of it. Maybe I don’t always call it that in my head, but that’s what it is. I’ll choose something lighter because it feels safer, like I’ll still be able to move the way I need to. With heavier food, I get this panicky feeling, like one meal is going to somehow make me bigger and slower, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “I’m skipping food because I’m scared of gaining weight,” because that sounds really blunt, but that’s kind of what it comes down to.\n\nIf the only thing around is something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I’ll put it off or just not have it. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ll say I’ll eat later, but really it’s because I get this panicky feeling like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Bigger and slower. And then I start thinking about work, being on ladders, moving through rooms, bending around furniture and buckets and drop cloths, and I just picture myself feeling clumsy or heavy in my body.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t change everything overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like just one meal. It feels like I’m risking losing the way I can move, if that makes sense. So yeah, I do avoid eating sometimes when food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavier.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because I’ll dress it up as, like, “I just don’t want to feel sluggish,” or “I need to be able to move for work,” but underneath that, yeah, it’s the fear of gaining weight or feeling bigger.\n\nLike if I’m looking at something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something really filling—I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a lot of food.” I get this panicky feeling, like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up the next day bigger and slower somehow. And then I picture myself at work, trying to get around ladders and drop cloths and furniture, or squeezing through rooms with paint cans and all my stuff, and I feel like my body won’t move the way I need it to.\n\nSo yes, I guess that is the main reason I restrict. It’s not that I’m trying to make some big statement about food. I’m on my feet all day, I’m moving around, and I don’t want to feel weighed down. So when someone says I’m too thin or they act alarmed, it’s hard for me to really take that in, because in my head it doesn’t feel like some emergency.\n\nI’m not saying I think I’m overweight exactly. It’s more like… I don’t see what everyone else seems to be so worried about. If it’s something that feels “safe” to me, I can sometimes just eat it and not have it take over my whole head.\n\nBut if it’s something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich or something that feels really dense, then yeah, it comes up almost immediately. It’s like I’m not even just thinking about the food anymore—I’m already imagining what it’s going to do to me. I get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up the next morning bigger, heavier, slower… like my body won’t move right.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic, because logically I know one meal isn’t supposed to transform you overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel logical. I start picturing myself at work trying to get around ladders and through rooms, bending and reaching and carrying things, and I just imagine feeling bulky or clumsy, like I won’t fit through the space the same way or won’t be able to move how I need to.\n\nSo… not every time I eat, exactly. But any time the food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavy, then yes, it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I look fat, exactly. That word feels… too simple for what happens in my head. It’s more like when other people say I’m too thin, I don’t really see it the way they do. I hear them, but it feels like they’re reacting to something much more dramatic than what I’m actually seeing or feeling.\n\nLike, to me, it doesn’t feel like my weight is this emergency everyone keeps acting like it is. And then when someone looks worried or says I need to gain weight, it almost feels like they’re misunderstanding it, like they’re making a huge deal out of something that, in my mind, is just me managing things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I look fat when they say I’m too thin. But I also don’t fully believe them, I guess. I don’t look at myself and think, “This is dangerous,” the way they seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don’t really think, “Oh, I look underweight,” no. I mean… I can tell I’m not big, obviously. I’m not saying I look the same as everyone else or anything like that. But I don’t see it as this alarming thing people keep describing.\n\nIt’s more like—I look at myself and I think, okay, I’m keeping myself in control, I’m staying disciplined, I’m managing with how busy I am and how physical my work can be. I’m moving around all day, and I don’t want to feel weighed down or like my body is getting in the way. So when other people act like my weight is some emergency, it doesn’t match what I’m seeing.\n\nI guess I can understand that maybe I look thinner than some people expect, but “underweight” sounds so serious. It sounds like something dangerous, and that’s not really how it feels to me when I look in the mirror. It feels like everyone else is making a big deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what other people see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I guess… not exactly. I mean, I know other people are seeing the same body, technically, but I don’t think they’re interpreting it the same way I am.\n\nWhen they look at me, it feels like they jump straight to concern, like my weight is some huge emergency or something that needs to be fixed right away. And when I look at myself, or when I think about my weight, it doesn’t land that way for me. It feels more like I’m just keeping things controlled. Like I’m busy, I’m working, I’m on my feet, I need to stay able to move and not feel weighed down. So when people act shocked or worried, I kind of feel like… you’re making this into a bigger thing than it is.\n\nI’m not saying they’re lying, exactly. I know they must see something that bothers them. But I don’t see it with that same urgency. To me it feels like they’re reacting to me being disciplined, or to me not eating the way they think I should, and turning it into this whole dramatic thing. So I guess my weight probably isn’t the same in my head as it is to them, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you want your body to look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, but not in the way people probably mean when they ask that. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking I want to look like some specific picture or be a certain clothing size. It’s more tied to how I imagine my body working, I guess.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something that feels really dense—I get this immediate panic that it’s going to change me overnight. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’ll wake up and my body will be bigger, slower, harder to move. And then I start thinking about work, because I’m on ladders, I’m moving around rooms, reaching, bending, carrying things, trying to get into corners. I can’t feel bulky or weighed down doing that. I need to feel light enough and in control enough to move the way I need to.\n\nSo I guess the fear is related to how I want my body to look, but it’s also about how I want it to feel. I don’t want to feel like my body is taking up more space or getting in the way. When I imagine gaining weight, I don’t just picture looking different—I picture being slower, less capable, like everything would be harder. That’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you think your body looks to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least partly. I don’t know if it’s only about how I think I look to other people, but I do think about it. Like, if I eat something heavy—pizza or a big sandwich or something like that—I get this panicky feeling almost immediately, like it’s going to show on me. Like I’m going to wake up bigger somehow, even if I know that probably sounds irrational when I say it out loud.\n\nAnd then I start imagining what that would mean when people see me, like they’d notice I was bigger or that I’d lost control or something. But it’s also not just the looking part. It’s like I picture being heavier and slower, like my body would move differently. With my work, I’m up and down ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit into awkward spaces. I need to feel light and able to move. So when I think about gaining weight, I imagine other people seeing me as bigger, yes, but I also imagine feeling clumsy or weighed down.\n\nSo… yes, it’s connected to how I think I might look to others, but it’s really tangled up with this fear that if I eat the wrong thing, my body will change and I won’t be able to move the way I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you perceive your own body shape or size?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s probably the closest way to say it. It’s very connected to how I perceive my own size, even if I don’t always trust that other people see it the same way.\n\nLike, if I eat something that feels heavy — pizza, or a big sandwich, or something with a lot of bread and cheese — I don’t just think, “Oh, I ate a big meal.” My mind goes straight to this feeling that my body is going to be different. Like I’ll wake up the next morning and somehow be bigger, puffier, slower… like my shape will have changed in a way I can feel and see. I know when I say that out loud it sounds like it shouldn’t happen that fast, but in the moment it feels really convincing.\n\nAnd it’s not just about looking in the mirror, though that’s part of it. It’s also this sense of my body taking up more space than it should. I start imagining myself moving around at work and feeling weighed down. I’m on ladders, I’m reaching up, bending, carrying things, squeezing around furniture or into corners, and I need my body to feel manageable. If I feel even a little heavier or bigger, I start thinking I won’t move right, like I’ll be slower or clumsier or just… not able to do what I need to do.\n\nSo yes, my fear of gaining weight is really tied to how I see my own body shape and size. It’s like I’m constantly trying to keep it from becoming something that feels too big or too heavy for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you think your body should look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if I have some clear idea of how my body “should” look, like a picture in my head exactly, but I do have this feeling that it should stay… light, I guess. Manageable. Not bulky or heavy or like it’s taking up too much space.\n\nWhen I eat something that feels heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, it’s not just that I feel full. I get this really panicky feeling, like I’ve done something that’s going to change my body right away. I’ll start thinking, what if I wake up tomorrow and I’m bigger? What if I feel slower? What if I can’t move the same way?\n\nAnd because of my work, that thought really gets to me. I’m climbing ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, working in corners or tight spots, and I need to feel like my body can do all of that without getting in the way. So when I think about how my body should look, it’s tied up with how I think it should function. Like it should be small enough or light enough that I can move easily and not feel weighed down.\n\nSo yes, the fear of gaining weight is related to that. It’s like I’m scared that if I eat the wrong thing, I’ll wake up in a body that feels too big or too slow for what I need it to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body is a different size or shape than it actually is?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t think I see myself as, like, wildly different from reality, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I look in the mirror and think I’m a completely different person or anything.\n\nBut at the same time, I don’t really know how accurate I am. Some days I’ll look at myself and feel like I’m bigger than I expected, or like certain parts of me are taking up more space, and then later I’m not sure if that was real or just how I was feeling in that moment. It can shift depending on what I’ve eaten or how my clothes feel or even just how tense I am.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I see my body as a different size than it actually is,” because I don’t have some outside measurement in my head to compare it to. But I also don’t fully trust my own perception all the time. It can feel very real to me in the moment, even if afterward I can question it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I think that’s the honest answer.\n\nPart of me wants to say yes, because I’m looking at my actual body, you know? It’s not like I’m imagining something out of nowhere. But then another part of me knows that how I see it changes a lot depending on the day, or what I’ve eaten, or how my clothes are sitting, or if I feel full or tense. So if it changes that much, then I guess I can’t be completely sure it’s accurate.\n\nIt’s hard because in the moment it feels accurate. Like if I feel bigger, I don’t experience it as “oh, this is just a feeling.” I experience it like it’s information about my body. Then later, sometimes I can step back and think, maybe that wasn’t totally reliable. But I don’t always have that distance when it’s happening.\n\nSo… I’m not sure my body image is accurate. I wouldn’t say I trust it completely. I think I treat it like it’s accurate a lot of the time, but I don’t actually know if it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe partly, but I don’t think it’s only that. It’s not always like I’m looking in the mirror and thinking, “I’m already too big,” exactly. It’s more like I’m scared I could become bigger really quickly, or that eating certain things will make my body feel different in a way I can’t undo.

Like if I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling afterward. I start thinking I’m going to wake up the next day bigger, or slower, or like my body won’t move the same way. And then I picture myself at work trying to climb ladders or move around rooms or reach into corners, and I worry I won’t be able to do it as easily. Like my body will feel in the way.

So I guess it’s less that I definitely see my body as larger than it is right now, and more that I’m afraid it’s going to become larger, and that if it does, I won’t be able to function the way I need to. That fear feels really immediate when I’ve eaten something that feels heavy.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… honestly, I’m here because the doctor at the clinic kind of got on me about my eating. I didn’t really think it was something I needed a whole appointment for, but they were pretty insistent.\n\nMost days I just keep things simple, you know? Like I’ll have coffee in the morning, especially if I’m heading to a job early, and then maybe I’ll grab a few bites of something between painting jobs if there’s time or if I remember. And then dinner is usually small, if I’m not too busy with class paperwork or too tired by the time I get home. It’s not like I’m trying to make it complicated—I just sort of have a routine, and food ends up being… not the main thing, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you enjoy eating when you do have the time for it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “enjoy” it exactly. I mean, sometimes if something tastes good, sure, I can recognize that it tastes good. Like if someone made something really nice or if there’s a specific thing I’m in the mood for, I’m not saying I’m incapable of liking flavors or anything.\n\nBut most of the time eating feels more like… a task? Like something I have to fit in around everything else. And sometimes by the time I sit down, I’m already thinking about what I still need to do, or whether I have enough time before I have to prep for the next job or finish paperwork, so it’s hard to really relax and enjoy it. I guess I don’t pay attention to it the way other people seem to. People talk about food like it’s this big enjoyable thing, and for me it’s just… I don’t know, it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess, yeah, a little. Like, the ones I wear on job sites, they kind of hang on me now, and I’m always pulling them up or tightening my belt more than I remember having to.\n\nAnd people have been commenting, which is honestly kind of irritating. Like coworkers or people on jobs will say, “You look too thin,” or ask if I’m eating enough, and I don’t really know what they want me to say to that. I feel like I’m just on my feet all day, climbing ladders, carrying stuff, moving around, so to me it makes sense that I’ve just… leaned out some. It doesn’t feel like this big dramatic thing the way other people make it sound.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “worried” sounds kind of dramatic, but yeah, I guess I am. Not like I sit around thinking about numbers all day, but if I eat something that feels heavy—like pizza, or a really big sandwich, something like that—I get this really uncomfortable panicky feeling afterward.\n\nIt’s like my brain goes straight to, okay, tomorrow I’m going to wake up and feel bigger, heavier, slower. And I know that probably sounds stupid when I say it out loud, because one meal shouldn’t do that, but in the moment it feels really real. I start thinking about work, like being up on ladders or squeezing around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans and trying to move quickly, and I just picture myself not being able to move the way I need to. Like I’ll be sluggish or clumsy or just… too much in my own body.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m trying to be difficult about food. It’s more that certain foods make me feel like I’m going to lose control of how I feel in my body, and then I get tense about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight when deciding what to eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, probably more than I want to admit. It’s not always like I’m sitting there calculating everything, but when I’m deciding what to eat, I definitely think about how it’s going to make me feel in my body.\n\nLike if it’s something heavier—pizza, or a big sandwich, or something really filling—I’ll hesitate. I start thinking, if I eat that, am I going to feel bigger afterward? Am I going to wake up tomorrow feeling heavy or slow? And then it sort of spirals from there, because my work is so physical. I’m on ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit around furniture and drop cloths and paint cans, and I hate the idea of feeling like my body is in the way.\n\nSo yeah, weight is part of it. Maybe I don’t always call it that in my head, but that’s what it is. I’ll choose something lighter because it feels safer, like I’ll still be able to move the way I need to. With heavier food, I get this panicky feeling, like one meal is going to somehow make me bigger and slower, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever avoid eating because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t always think of it as “I’m skipping food because I’m scared of gaining weight,” because that sounds really blunt, but that’s kind of what it comes down to.\n\nIf the only thing around is something that feels really heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I’ll put it off or just not have it. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ll say I’ll eat later, but really it’s because I get this panicky feeling like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Bigger and slower. And then I start thinking about work, being on ladders, moving through rooms, bending around furniture and buckets and drop cloths, and I just picture myself feeling clumsy or heavy in my body.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational, because one meal shouldn’t change everything overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel like just one meal. It feels like I’m risking losing the way I can move, if that makes sense. So yeah, I do avoid eating sometimes when food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavier.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it probably is. I don’t always say it that clearly to myself, because I’ll dress it up as, like, “I just don’t want to feel sluggish,” or “I need to be able to move for work,” but underneath that, yeah, it’s the fear of gaining weight or feeling bigger.\n\nLike if I’m looking at something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something really filling—I don’t just think, “Oh, that’s a lot of food.” I get this panicky feeling, like if I eat it, I’m going to wake up the next day bigger and slower somehow. And then I picture myself at work, trying to get around ladders and drop cloths and furniture, or squeezing through rooms with paint cans and all my stuff, and I feel like my body won’t move the way I need it to.\n\nSo yes, I guess that is the main reason I restrict. It’s not that I’m trying to make some big statement about food. It’s more like I’m trying to avoid that panic, that feeling that I’m going to become heavier and not be able to function right in my body.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look overweight now?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I look overweight now. I mean, I know other people act like my weight is this huge, urgent problem, but when I look at myself, that’s not what I see.\n\nTo me it feels like people are making a really big deal out of something that’s more about me being disciplined, or being busy, or just keeping myself able to work the way I need to. I’m on my feet all day, I’m moving around, and I don’t want to feel weighed down. So when someone says I’m too thin or they act alarmed, it’s hard for me to really take that in, because in my head it doesn’t feel like some emergency.\n\nI’m not saying I think I’m overweight exactly. It’s more like… I don’t see what everyone else seems to be so worried about. I feel like they’re looking at me and deciding it’s worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about the possibility of gaining weight every time you eat?\n\nPatient: Not every single time, no… or at least I don’t think so. If it’s something that feels “safe” to me, I can sometimes just eat it and not have it take over my whole head.\n\nBut if it’s something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich or something that feels really dense, then yeah, it comes up almost immediately. It’s like I’m not even just thinking about the food anymore—I’m already imagining what it’s going to do to me. I get this panicky feeling that I’ll wake up the next morning bigger, heavier, slower… like my body won’t move right.\n\nAnd I know that sounds dramatic, because logically I know one meal isn’t supposed to transform you overnight. But in the moment it doesn’t feel logical. I start picturing myself at work trying to get around ladders and through rooms, bending and reaching and carrying things, and I just imagine feeling bulky or clumsy, like I won’t fit through the space the same way or won’t be able to move how I need to.\n\nSo… not every time I eat, exactly. But any time the food feels like it could make me gain weight or feel heavy, then yes, it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look fat even when others say you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say I look fat, exactly. That word feels… too simple for what happens in my head. It’s more like when other people say I’m too thin, I don’t really see it the way they do. I hear them, but it feels like they’re reacting to something much more dramatic than what I’m actually seeing or feeling.\n\nLike, to me, it doesn’t feel like my weight is this emergency everyone keeps acting like it is. It feels more like I’m just being disciplined, or I’m busy, or I’m trying to keep myself able to function and move around for work. And then when someone looks worried or says I need to gain weight, it almost feels like they’re misunderstanding it, like they’re making a huge deal out of something that, in my mind, is just me managing things.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I look fat when they say I’m too thin. But I also don’t fully believe them, I guess. I don’t look at myself and think, “This is dangerous,” the way they seem to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I don’t really think, “Oh, I look underweight,” no. I mean… I can tell I’m not big, obviously. I’m not saying I look the same as everyone else or anything like that. But I don’t see it as this alarming thing people keep describing.\n\nIt’s more like—I look at myself and I think, okay, I’m keeping myself in control, I’m staying disciplined, I’m managing with how busy I am and how physical my work can be. I’m moving around all day, and I don’t want to feel weighed down or like my body is getting in the way. So when other people act like my weight is some emergency, it doesn’t match what I’m seeing.\n\nI guess I can understand that maybe I look thinner than some people expect, but “underweight” sounds so serious. It sounds like something dangerous, and that’s not really how it feels to me when I look in the mirror. It feels like everyone else is making a big deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what other people see when they look at you?\n\nPatient: I guess… not exactly. I mean, I know other people are seeing the same body, technically, but I don’t think they’re interpreting it the same way I am.\n\nWhen they look at me, it feels like they jump straight to concern, like my weight is some huge emergency or something that needs to be fixed right away. And when I look at myself, or when I think about my weight, it doesn’t land that way for me. It feels more like I’m just keeping things controlled. Like I’m busy, I’m working, I’m on my feet, I need to stay able to move and not feel weighed down. So when people act shocked or worried, I kind of feel like… you’re making this into a bigger thing than it is.\n\nI’m not saying they’re lying, exactly. I know they must see something that bothers them. But I don’t see it with that same urgency. To me it feels like they’re reacting to me being disciplined, or to me not eating the way they think I should, and turning it into this whole dramatic thing. So I guess my weight probably isn’t the same in my head as it is to them, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you want your body to look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, but not in the way people probably mean when they ask that. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking I want to look like some specific picture or be a certain clothing size. It’s more tied to how I imagine my body working, I guess.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy—pizza, or a big sandwich, something that feels really dense—I get this immediate panic that it’s going to change me overnight. I know that probably sounds dramatic, but in the moment it feels very real, like I’ll wake up and my body will be bigger, slower, harder to move. And then I start thinking about work, because I’m on ladders, I’m moving around rooms, reaching, bending, carrying things, trying to get into corners. I can’t feel bulky or weighed down doing that. I need to feel light enough and in control enough to move the way I need to.\n\nSo I guess the fear is related to how I want my body to look, but it’s also about how I want it to feel. I don’t want to feel like my body is taking up more space or getting in the way. When I imagine gaining weight, I don’t just picture looking different—I picture being slower, less capable, like everything would be harder. That’s what scares me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you think your body looks to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least partly. I don’t know if it’s only about how I think I look to other people, but I do think about it. Like, if I eat something heavy—pizza or a big sandwich or something like that—I get this panicky feeling almost immediately, like it’s going to show on me. Like I’m going to wake up bigger somehow, even if I know that probably sounds irrational when I say it out loud.\n\nAnd then I start imagining what that would mean when people see me, like they’d notice I was bigger or that I’d lost control or something. But it’s also not just the looking part. It’s like I picture being heavier and slower, like my body would move differently. With my work, I’m up and down ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, carrying things, trying to fit into awkward spaces. I need to feel light and able to move. So when I think about gaining weight, I imagine other people seeing me as bigger, yes, but I also imagine feeling clumsy or weighed down.\n\nSo… yes, it’s connected to how I think I might look to others, but it’s really tangled up with this fear that if I eat the wrong thing, my body will change and I won’t be able to move the way I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you perceive your own body shape or size?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think that’s probably the closest way to say it. It’s very connected to how I perceive my own size, even if I don’t always trust that other people see it the same way.\n\nLike, if I eat something that feels heavy — pizza, or a big sandwich, or something with a lot of bread and cheese — I don’t just think, “Oh, I ate a big meal.” My mind goes straight to this feeling that my body is going to be different. Like I’ll wake up the next morning and somehow be bigger, puffier, slower… like my shape will have changed in a way I can feel and see. I know when I say that out loud it sounds like it shouldn’t happen that fast, but in the moment it feels really convincing.\n\nAnd it’s not just about looking in the mirror, though that’s part of it. It’s also this sense of my body taking up more space than it should. I start imagining myself moving around at work and feeling weighed down. I’m on ladders, I’m reaching up, bending, carrying things, squeezing around furniture or into corners, and I need my body to feel manageable. If I feel even a little heavier or bigger, I start thinking I won’t move right, like I’ll be slower or clumsier or just… not able to do what I need to do.\n\nSo yes, my fear of gaining weight is really tied to how I see my own body shape and size. It’s like I’m constantly trying to keep it from becoming something that feels too big or too heavy for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight gain fears are related to how you think your body should look?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t know if I have some clear idea of how my body “should” look, like a picture in my head exactly, but I do have this feeling that it should stay… light, I guess. Manageable. Not bulky or heavy or like it’s taking up too much space.\n\nWhen I eat something that feels heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, it’s not just that I feel full. I get this really panicky feeling, like I’ve done something that’s going to change my body right away. I’ll start thinking, what if I wake up tomorrow and I’m bigger? What if I feel slower? What if I can’t move the same way?\n\nAnd because of my work, that thought really gets to me. I’m climbing ladders, moving around rooms, bending, reaching, working in corners or tight spots, and I need to feel like my body can do all of that without getting in the way. So when I think about how my body should look, it’s tied up with how I think it should function. Like it should be small enough or light enough that I can move easily and not feel weighed down.\n\nSo yes, the fear of gaining weight is related to that. It’s like I’m scared that if I eat the wrong thing, I’ll wake up in a body that feels too big or too slow for what I need it to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body is a different size or shape than it actually is?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t think I see myself as, like, wildly different from reality, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I look in the mirror and think I’m a completely different person or anything.\n\nBut at the same time, I don’t really know how accurate I am. Some days I’ll look at myself and feel like I’m bigger than I expected, or like certain parts of me are taking up more space, and then later I’m not sure if that was real or just how I was feeling in that moment. It can shift depending on what I’ve eaten or how my clothes feel or even just how tense I am.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t confidently say, “Yes, I see my body as a different size than it actually is,” because I don’t have some outside measurement in my head to compare it to. But I also don’t fully trust my own perception all the time. It can feel very real to me in the moment, even if afterward I can question it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I think that’s the honest answer.\n\nPart of me wants to say yes, because I’m looking at my actual body, you know? It’s not like I’m imagining something out of nowhere. But then another part of me knows that how I see it changes a lot depending on the day, or what I’ve eaten, or how my clothes are sitting, or if I feel full or tense. So if it changes that much, then I guess I can’t be completely sure it’s accurate.\n\nIt’s hard because in the moment it feels accurate. Like if I feel bigger, I don’t experience it as “oh, this is just a feeling.” I experience it like it’s information about my body. Then later, sometimes I can step back and think, maybe that wasn’t totally reliable. But I don’t always have that distance when it’s happening.\n\nSo… I’m not sure my body image is accurate. I wouldn’t say I trust it completely. I think I treat it like it’s accurate a lot of the time, but I don’t actually know if it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you see your body as larger than it actually is?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, but I don’t think it’s only that. It’s not always like I’m looking in the mirror and thinking, “I’m already too big,” exactly. It’s more like I’m scared I could become bigger really quickly, or that eating certain things will make my body feel different in a way I can’t undo.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling afterward. I start thinking I’m going to wake up the next day bigger, or slower, or like my body won’t move the same way. And then I picture myself at work trying to climb ladders or move around rooms or reach into corners, and I worry I won’t be able to do it as easily. Like my body will feel in the way.\n\nSo I guess it’s less that I definitely see my body as larger than it is right now, and more that I’m afraid it’s going to become larger, and that if it does, I won’t be able to function the way I need to. That fear feels really immediate when I’ve eaten something that feels heavy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
